to manage and execute one program or task at
a time.

1. Single-user Single-tasking                              3. Multi-user Multi-tasking

at a time.                                                 allowing each user to run multiple tasks

                                                           lsÍug bv i,ik w;ru tljr nyq mßYS,lhskag
These systems are foundational in the history of           o iyh fõ'
computing and are characterized by their
simplicity and efficiency for specific, limited-use        NOTE :
scenarios.                                                 A multi-user single-tasking operating system
fuu moaO;s mß.Kl b;sydifha moku jk w;r                     cannot exist because when multiple users are
úfYaIs;" iSñ; Ndú; wjia:d i|yd tAjdfha ir,                 active, the system must handle multiple tasks at

How data is physically stored on disk                     Sectors

                                                          Sectors are small sections of a track. They are

Blocks are made when the user formats the disk.                    HDD

But block size can be tuned.
mßYS,lhd ;eáh wdlD;slrKh lrk úg ldKav

                                                            third block is wasted.

size, the last block allocated to that file will not

Path Name
f.dkq ud¾. kduh

A file pathname is the complete address or path
that specifies the location of a file or directory
in a file system.
f.dkq ud¾. kduhla hkq f.dkq moaO;shl
f.dkqjl fyda kdudj,shl msysàu ksYaÑ;j olajk
iïmQ¾K ,smskh fyda ud¾.hhs'

file, starting from the root directory.
thg uQ, kdudj,sfhka wdrïN ù f.dkqjg uÕ                      Ex.01
fmkajk ish¨u kdudj,s ^f*da,avr& we;=<;a fõ'                 In HTML, as xyz.jpg is in the same folder as
                                                            abc.html, it can be referred to simply as
1. Absolute Path name                                       xyz.jpg. This is a relative path name.
   ksrfmalaI ud¾. kduh
                                                            HTML ys" xyz.jpg abc.html f*da,avrfhau ;sfí
                                                            ksid" th ir,j xyz.jpg f,i ye¢kaúh yel'
Shows the complete directory path starting                  fuh idfmalaI ud¾. kduhls'
from the root directory. It begins with the root
(such as/in Unix-like systems or C: in                      Ex.02
Windows) and includes all folders up to the                 Since cheems.jpg is stored in the images folder
required file or directory.                                 and used in pqr.html, it can be referenced as
uQ, kdudj,sfhka wdrïN ù iïmQ¾K kdudj,s

FAT Chaining Questions (MCQ)

 01. The block size of a disk is 8KB. A portion of its File Allocation Table (FAT) at a particular time is
 shown below. The portion shown indicates the blocks of the fitgirl-bins.md5 file as well.
 ;eáhl íf,dla fldgil úYd,;ajh 8KB fõ' ksYaÑ; fõ,djl tys f.dkq úNdck j.=fõ (FAT) fldgila
 my; oelafõ' fmkajd we;s fldgfia fitgirl-bins.md5 f.dkqfõ fldgia o olajhs'
                                                                                     FAT
 Notes:                                                                              150 151

 I. The last block of a file is indicated by -1.                                     151 152
 f.dkqjl wjidk fldgi -1 u.ska oelafõ'
                                                                                     152 -1
 II. The directory entry of a file contains the block number of the first            153 154
 block of the file.
 f.dkqjl kdudj,s f;dr;=r f.dkqfõ m<uq fldgfia ldKav wxlh wvx.=                       154 155
 fõ'
                                                                                     155 156

 Which of the following gives the directory entry for the fitgirl-bins.md5 file and the disk space
 allocated for the fitgirl-bins.md5 file respectively?
 fitgirl-bins.md5 f.dkqj i|yd kdudj,s f;dr;=r (directory entry) iy fitgirl-bins.md5 f.dkqj
 i|yd fjka lr we;s ;eá bv ms<sfj,ska ,nd fokafka my; i|yka ljrla o@

 1) 150, 16KB               2) 150, 24KB             3) 151, 16KB
 4) 152, 8KB                5) 150, 32KB

 Answer: 2)

 The block size of the disk is 8KB, which means each block can store 8KB of data. The FAT table shows
 how different blocks are linked for a file. The number in each row corresponds to the next block of
 the file. If the value is -1, it indicates that it's the last block of the file.
 ;eáfha íf,dla fldgil m%udKh 8KB fõ" tkï iEu íf,dla fldgil tlgu 8KB o;a; .nvd l< yel'
 f.dkq úNdck j.=j (FAT) u.ska f.dkqjla i|yd úúO íf,dla fldgia iïnkaO jk wdldrh fmkajhs' tla
 tla fma<sfha wxlh f.dkqfõ B<. fldgig wkqrEm fõ' w.h -1 kï" th f.dkqfõ wjidk fldgi nj
 fmkakqï lrhs'

 According to Note II, the directory entry for the file is the first block number, which is 150.
 Note II g wkqj" f.dkqj i|yd kdudj,s f;dr;=r m<uq jdrK wxlfha ioyka jk w;r th 150 fõ'

 This means that fitgirl-bins.md5 uses three blocks (150, 151, 152).
 fuhska woyia lrkafka fitgirl-bins.md5 íf,dla fldgia ;=kla (150, 151, 152) Ndú;d lrk njhs'

                                                    51
                                                                                                        ictfromabc.com

                                                                               FAT
•   Each block is 8KB.
    iEu íf,dla fldgilau 8KB fõ'                                                150 151 STEP 3
                                                                               151 152 STEP 2
•   Since the file uses 3 blocks, the total disk space used by the file is     152      -1   STEP 1
    8KB×3 = 24KB.
    f.dkqj íf,dla fldgia 3la Ndú;d lrk neúka" f.dkqj Ndú;d lrk                 153 154
    iïmQ¾K ;eáfha bv m%udKh 8KB×3 = 24KB fõ'
                                                                               154 155
                                                                               155 156

The directory entry is block 150. The total disk space allocated is 24KB.

Thus, the correct answer is option 2) 150, 24KB.

Answer : 5 )

can’t find the starting block of it.

Identifying the Directory Entry
kdudj,s m%fõYh y÷kd .ekSu

According to Note 2, the directory entry is the block number of the first block of the file. From
the table, the first block of report.txt is 310.
igyk 2 g wkqj kudj,s m%fõYh hkq f.dkqfõ m<uq fldgfia jdrK wxlhhs' j.=fjka" report.txt ys

    iEu íf,dla fldgilau 8KB fõ'

2. Shortest Job First (SJF)                            •   Preemptive SJF

A scheduling algorithm that selects the process        If a new process arrives with a shorter remaining
with the shortest burst time to execute next.          time, the CPU preempts (interrupts) the current
B<Õg l%shd;aul lsÍug fláu iys; l%shdhkh                process.
f;dard.kakd kshulrK we,af.dß;uhls'                     oekg l%shd;aul jk l%shdj,shg jvd wvq b;sß
                                                       ld,hla iys; kj l%shdj,shla moaO;shg
It can be either preemptive or non-preemptive          meñ‚hfyd;a" CPU tl oekg mj;sk l%shdj,sh
th preemptive fyda non-preemptive úh yelsh             k;r lr kj l%shdj,shg udre fõ'

• Non-Preemptive SJF                                   Always runs the process with the least
Once a process starts execution, it cannot be          remaining time.
interrupted.                                            fuh ieuúgu l%shd;aul jkafka wju b;sß
jrla l%shdj,shla l%shd;aul ùug mgka .;a miq" th        ld,hla iys; l%shdj,shhs
w;ruÕ§ keje;aúh fkdyel'
                                                       The preemptive version of SJF called Shortest
The CPU completes the shortest job fully before        Remaining Time First (SRTF)
switching.                                             SJF ys preemptive wdldrh Shortest Remaining
fjk;a l%shdj,shlg udre ùug fmr" CPU tl                 Time First (SRTF) f,i ye¢kafõ'

                                                          What is Starvation?
                                                          A situation where a process waits indefinitely in
                                                          the ready queue because higher-priority
                                                          processes continuously get the CPU. Aging is
                                                          the solution for that.
                                                          by< m%uqL;d l%shdj,Ska wLKavj CPU ,nd .kakd
                                                          ksid" l%shdj,shla iQodkï fmda,sfï Èk kshuhla
                                                          fkdue;sj /£ isák ;;ajhla' ld,h;a iuÕ
                                                          m%uqL;djh jeä lsÍu tA i|yd úi÷uhs'

                                                          What is Aging?
                                                          A technique where the priority of long-waited
                                                          processes in the system is gradually increased
                                                          by 1 for every 15 minutes.
                                                          moaO;sfha È.=ld,Skj /§ isák l%shdhk j,
                                                          m%uqL;djh iEu ñks;a;= 15 lg jrlau 1 lska
                                                          l%ufhka jeä jk ;dËKhls'

Calculations from the schedulers / kshulrK .Kkh lsÍï'

Consider the following four processes with their respective arrival times, burst durations and priorities
for all the calculations.
ish¨u .Kkh lsÍï i|yd wod< meñŒfï fõ,djka" l%shd;aul jk ld,iSudjka iy m%uqL;d iuÕ my;
l%shdj,s y;r i,ld n,kak'

       Process           Arrival Time (ms)               Burst Duration (ms)              Priority
          P1                      0                                 5                        3
          P2                      1                                 3                        1
          P3                      2                                 8                        4
          P4                      4                                 4                        2

To calculate the turnaround duration of a process, we have to subtract the time at which that process
arrived which is the arrival time from the time at which that process was completed which is the
completion time.
l%shdhkhl turnaround duration tl .Kkh lsÍu i|yd" tu l%shdhkh l%shd;aul ù wjika jQ ld,fhka
tkï completion time tflka tu l%shdhkh meñ‚ ld,h tkï arrival time tl wvq l< hq;=h' '

To calculate the waiting duration of a process, we have to subtract the burst duration which is the
duration that a particular process runs on the CPU from the turnaround duration.
l%shdj,shl waiting duration tl .Kkh lsÍu i|yd" hï lshdhkhla ilikh u; l%shd;aul jk
ld,iSudj jk burst duration tl turnaround duration tflka wvq l< hq;=h'

1. First-come, first-serve (FCFS)

 FCFS works by executing processes in the order they arrive, where the process that arrives first
 gets the CPU first and runs until it finishes.
 FCFS l%shd lrkafka l%shdj,s meñfKk wkqms<sfj,g l%shd;aul lsÍfuks" tys§ m<uqj meñfKk l%shdj,sh
 m<uqj CPU ,ndf.k th wjika jk f;la l%shd;aul fõ'

 Calculate the average turnaround duration and waiting duration.
 idudkH turnaround duration tl iy waiting duration tl .Kkh lrkak.

 First, we have to draw the Gantt chart
 m,uqfjka" ld, rduqj weÈh hq;=h

                                                   89
                                                                                                      ictfromabc.com

P1 arrives at time 0 and starts execution immediately. It runs for 5 units of time.
P1" ld, 0 § meñK jydu l%shd;aul lsÍu wdrïN lrhs' th ld, tAll 5 la ;siafia Odjkh fõ'

P2 arrives at time 1, but it has to wait until P1 finishes. P2 starts execution at time 5 and runs for 3
units of time.
P2" ld, 1 yS§ meñfKa" kuq;a thg P1 wjika jk f;la /£ isàug isÿ fõ' P2 l%shd;aul lsÍu ld, 5
ys§ wdrïN lrk w;r ld, tAll 3la ;siafia th l%shd;aul fõ'

P3 arrives at time 2, but it has to wait until P2 finishes. P3 starts execution at time 8 and runs for 8
units of time.
P3" ld, 2 yS§ fõ,djg meñfKa" kuq;a thg P2 wjika jk f;la /£ isàug isÿ fõ' P3" ld, 8 yS§
l%shd;aul lsÍu wdrïN lrk w;r ld, tAll 8la ;siafia th l%shd;aul fõ'

P4 arrives at time 4, but it has to wait until P3 finishes. P4 starts execution at time 16 and runs for
4 units of time.
P4" ld, 4 yS§ meñfKa" kuq;a thg P3 wjika jk f;la /£ isàug isÿ fõ' P4" ld, 16 yS§ l%shd;aul
lsÍu wdrïN lrk w;r ld, tAll 4la ;siafi th l%shd;aul fõ'

Now, we can calculate the turnaround and waiting durations of each process as follows.
oeka" wmg tla tla l%shdhkfha turnaround iy waiting durations my; mßÈ .Kkh l< yel'

  Process     Arrival Time      Completion       Burst Duration       Turnaround           Waiting
    ID            (ms)            Time                (ms)             Duration            Duration
    P1             0                5                   5              5–0=5               5–5=0
    P2              1                8                  3               8–1=7               7–3=4
    P3              2                16                 8              16 – 2 = 14         14 – 8 = 6
    P4              4                20                 4              20 – 4 = 16        16 – 4 = 12

      Average turnaround duration (ATD):                Average waiting duration (AWD):

      ATD = (5 + 7 + 14 + 16) / 4                       AWD = (0 + 4 + 6 + 12) / 4
      ATD = 10.5                                        AWD = 5.5

                                                                                                        ictfromabc.com

2. Shortest Job First (SJF)
   • Non-Preemptive

  In Non-Preemptive SJF, once a process starts executing, it runs to completion without being
  interrupted.
  Non-Preemptive SJF yS§" l%shdhkhla l%shd;aul ùug mgka .;a miq" th ndOdjlska f;drj wjidkh
  olajd Èj hhs'

  Note:
  Here, it is assumed that the arrival time of each process is the same.
  fuys§ tla tla l%shdhkfha meñŒfï fõ,dj iudk nj Wml,amkh flfra'

  Calculate the average waiting duration of these four processes.
  fuu l%shdhk y;frys idudkH waiting duration tl .Kkh lrkak'

  Gantt chart:

  P2 is selected first because it has the shortest burst duration of 3ms. It completes execution at 3ms.
  P2 uq,skau f;dard.kq ,nkafka thg 3ms l fláu burst duration we;s neúks' th 3ms § l%shd;aul lsÍu
  iïmQ¾K lrhs'

  P4 is selected next with a burst duration of 4ms. It starts at 3ms and completes at 7ms.
  P4 ó<Õg 4 ms l burst duration tlla iuÕ f;dard .kq ,efí' th 3ms § wdrïN jk w;r 7ms §
  wjika fõ'

  P1 is selected next with a burst duration of 5ms. It starts at 7ms and completes at 12ms.
  P1 ó<Õg f;dard.kq ,nkafka 5ms l burst duration tlla iuÕsks' th 7ms § wdrïN jk w;r 12ms §
  wjika fõ'

  P3 is selected last with the longest burst duration of 8ms. It starts at 12ms and completes at 20ms.
  P3 wjika jrg f;dard.kq ,nkafka 8ms l §¾>;u burst duration tl iuÕsks' th 12ms § wdrïN jk
  w;r 20ms § wjika fõ'

  Let’s assume that each process arrived at 0ms.
  iEu l%shdhklau 0ms § meñ‚fha hehs is;uq'

    Process      Arrival Time    Completion      Burst Duration       Turnaround           Waiting
      ID             (ms)          Time               (ms)              Duration          Duration
      P1              0             12                  5              12 – 0 = 12        12 – 5 = 7
      P2              0               3                 3               3–0=3              3–3=0
      P3              0               20                8              20 – 0 = 20        20 – 8 = 12
      P4              0               7                 4               7–0=7              7–4=3

  Average Turnaround Duration: (3 + 7 + 12 + 20) / 4 = 10.5ms

  Average Waiting Duration: (0 + 3 + 7 + 12) / 4 = 5.5ms

If you wonder what happens if the arrival times of those processes were different, it is the same
answer that we get because this is Non-Preemptive SJF algorithm.
tu l%shdhkhkaf.a meñŒfï fõ,djka fjkia jqjfyd;a l=ula isÿfõoehs Tn l,amkd lrkafka kï" fuh
Non-Preemptive SJF we,af.dß;uhla neúka wmg ,efnkafka tlu ms<s;=rhs'

In Non-Preemptive SJF, even if a shorter process arrives when a longer process is already on the
CPU, the longer process will be executed to its completion without being interrupted by that
shorter process.
Non-Preemptive SJF j,§" È.= l%shdhkhla oekgu;a CPU u; we;s úg flá l%shdhkhla meñ‚ho" tu
flá l%shdhkh u.ska ndOdjlska f;drj È.= l%shdhkh tys wjidkh olajd l%shd;aul fõ'

    •     Preemptive SJF

In Preempive SJF, if a new process arrives with a shorter burst duration than the remaining
duration of the currently running process, the current process is preempted and the new
process is scheduled.
Preempive SJF, ys§" oekg l%shd;aul jk l%shdhkfha b;sß ù we;s burst duration tlg jvd flá
burst duration tlla iys; kj l%shdhkhla meñ‚hfyd;a" j;auka l%shdhkh mQ¾jfhka bj;a lr kj
l%shdhkh kshulrkq we;'

• P3 finishes execution.
   P3 l%shd;aul lsÍu wjika lrhs'

According to the above explanation, we can now calculate the turnaround and waiting duration of
each process.
by; meyeÈ,s lsÍu wkqj" wmg oeka tla tla l%shdhkfha turnaround iy waiting duration .Kkh l<
yelsh'

    Process   Arrival Time   Completion        Burst Duration   Turnaround         Waiting
      ID          (ms)         Time                 (ms)         Duration          Duration
      P1           0             8                    5          8–0=8             8–5=3
      P2           1               4                 3            4–1=3            3–3=0
      P3           2               20                8          20 – 2 = 18       18 – 8 = 10
      P4           4               12                4           12 – 4 = 8        8–4=4

Average Turnaround Duration (ATD): (8 + 3 + 18 + 8) / 4 = 9.25ms

Average Waiting Duration: (3 + 0 + 10 + 4) / 4 = 4.25ms

3. Priority Scheduling
   • Non-Preemptive

 Calculate the average waiting duration.
 idudkH waiting duration tl .Kkh lrkak'

 Gantt chart:

 P1 starts first because it is the only process available at time 0.
 P1 uq,skau wdrïN jkafka ld,h 0 jk úg mj;sk tlu l%shdhkh th jk neúks'

 P2 arrives at time 1 but doesn't start immediately because P1 is already running (non-preemptive).
 Once P1 finishes, P2 starts executing due to its highest priority (1).
 P2" ld,h 1 jk úg meñfKk kuq;a P1 oekgu;a l%shd;aul fjñka mj;sk ksid tu wjia:dfõ wdrïN
 fkdfõ ^non-preemptive&' P1 wjika jQ miq" P2 ys by<u m%uqL;djh 1 ksid th l%shd;aul ùug mgka
 .kS'

 P4 has a higher priority than P3 and starts executing after P2 finishes.
 P4 yg P3 g jvd by< m%uqL;djhla we;s w;r P2 wjika jQ miq th l%shd;aul ùug mgka .kS'

 Finally, P3 with the lowest priority starts last and completes at time 20.
 wjidk jYfhka" wvqu m%uqL;djh iys; P3 wjika jrg wdrïN ù ld,h 20 jk úg iïmQ¾K fõ'

     P3          2              20             8         20 – 2 = 18    18 – 8 = 10        4
     P4          4              12             4          12 – 4 = 8      8–4=4            2

 Average Waiting Duration: (0 + 4 + 10 + 4) / 4 = 4.5ms

4. Round Robin Algorithm

 Consider the set of 4 processes that are given in the table, arriving at time 0, with the burst
 durations given in milliseconds and the time quantum taken as 4 milliseconds for RR scheduling.
 ñ,s ;;amr j,ska ,nd § we;s burst durations iy RR kshu lsÍu i|yd ñ,s ;;amr 4la f,i .kakd ,o
 time quantum tlla iuÕ" § we;s j.=fõ we;s ld,h 0 § meñfKk l%shdhk 4la i,ld n,kak'

 Calculate the average waiting and turnaround durations of these 4 processes.
 fuu l%shdhk 4ys idudkH waiting iy turnaround durations .Kkh lrkak'

 Gantt chart:

 In this Round Robin scheduling example with a time quantum of 4ms, four processes P1, P2, P3 and
 P4 all arrive at time 0.
 fuu rjqkaâ frdìka kshu lsÍfï WodyrKfha§ time quantum tl 4ms la jk" P1" P2, P3 iy P4 l%shdhk
 y;r ld,h 0 § meñfKa'

 P1 starts execution from time 0 to 4ms and is preempted with 1ms remaining.
 P1" 0ms isg 4ms olajd olajd Odjkh jk w;r 1ms b;sßj ;sìh§ ilikfhka bj;a fõ'

 Next, P2 runs from time 4 to 7ms.
 ó<Õg" P2" 4ms isg 7ms olajd Odjkh lrhss'

 Then, P3 runs from time 7 to 11 ms and is preempted with 4ms remaining.
 bkamiqj" P3" 7ms isg 11ms olajd Odjkh jk w;r 4ms b;sßj ;sìh§ ilikfhka bj;a fõ'

 Next, P4 runs from time 11 to 15ms.
 ó<Õg" P4" 11ms isg 15ms olajd Odjkh lrhss'

 P1 resumes and completes its remaining 1ms from time 15 to 16ms.
 P1 kej; wdrïN lr tys b;sß 1ms l ld,h 15ms isg 16ms olajd iïmQ¾K lrhs'

 Finally, P3 resumes and completes its remaining 4ms from time 16 to 20ms.
 wjidk jYfhka" P3 kej; wdrïN lr tys b;sß 4ms l ld,h 16ms isg 20ms olajd iïmQ¾K lrhs'

 To calculate the turnaround and waiting durations, we can use the previous two formulas.
 Turnaround iy waiting durations .Kkh lsÍu i|yd" wmg fmr iQ;% fol Ndú;d l< yelsh'

    Process    Arrival time      Completion          Burst Duration   Turnaround Time     Waiting Time
      ID          (ms)           Time (ms)                (ms)              (ms)              (ms)
      P1             0                16                   5             16 – 0 = 16       16 – 5 = 11
      P2             0                 7                   3              7–0=7             7–3=4
      P3             0                20                   8             20 – 0 = 20       20 – 8 = 12
      P4             0                15                   4             15 – 0 = 15       15 – 4 = 11

 Average Turnaround Duration: (16 + 7 + 20 + 15) / 4 = 14.5ms

 Average Waiting Duration: (11 + 4 + 12 + 11) / 4 = 9.5ms

 Address    Maximum Addressable
 Bits       Memory
 1-bit      2¹ = 2 bytes
 2-bits     2² = 4 bytes
 3-bits     2³ = 8 bytes
 8-bits     2⁸ = 256 bytes
 10-bits    2¹⁰ bytes = 1 KB
 15-bits    2¹⁵ bytes = 2⁵ × 2¹⁰ = 32 × 1 KB
            = 32 KB
 20-bits    2²⁰ bytes = 1 MB
 24-bits    2²⁴ bytes = 2⁴ × 2²⁰ = 16 × 1 MB
            = 16 MB
 30-bits    2³⁰ bytes = 1 GB
 36-bits    2³⁶ bytes = 2⁶ × 2³⁰ = 64 × 1 GB
            = 64 GB

01. A computer system has virtual memory that is divided into 4096 pages. If one page is 4 MB of
    size, what is the total capacity of this virtual memory?
    mß.Kl moaO;shl w;:H u;lhla we;s w;r th msgq 4096 lg fnod we;' tla msgj         q l m%udKh 4
    MB kï" fuu w;:H u;lfha iïmQ¾K Odß;dj fldmuKo@
    Virtual memory capacity = Size of a page x number of pages
                            = 4 MB x 4096 = 22 x 210 x 210 Bytes x 212
                            = 222 x 212 Bytes = 24 x 230 Bytes
                            = 24 x Giga Bytes = 16 GB
02. A virtual memory consisting of 1024 pages has a size of 4 GB. What is the size of a single page?
    msgq 1024 lska iukaú; w;:H u;lhl m%udKh 4 GB fõ' ;ks msgqjl m%udKh fldmuKo@
    Size of one page = Virtual memory capacity / number of pages
                     = 4 GB / 1024 = 22 x 210 x 210 x 210 Bytes / 210
                     = 22 x 210 x 210 x 210 Bytes / 210 = 22 x 220 Bytes
                     = 22 x Mega Bytes
                     = 4 MB
03. A 2GB virtual memory has been divided into 512 KB size-pages. How many pages is this virtual
    memory divided into?
    2GB w;:H u;lhla 512 KB m%udKfha msgq j,g fnod we;' fuu w;:H u;lh msgq lShlg fnod
    ;sfío@
    Number of pages = Virtual memory capacity / Size of one page
                   = 2 GB / 512 KB = 21 x 210 x 210 x 210 Bytes / 29 x 210 Bytes
                   = 21 x 210 x 210 x 210 Bytes / 29 x 210 Bytes
                   = 21 x 210 x 210 / 29 = 21 x 210 x 21
                   = 22 x 210 = 212
                   = 4096
04. A system uses pages of size 8 KB. How many bits are required for the offset?
    moaO;shla 8 KB m%udKfha msgq Ndú;d lrhs' fkrej i|yd ìgq lShla wjYH fõo@
     2 offset bits = page size in bytes
                   = 8 KB = 8 x 210 Bytes
                   = 23 x 210 Bytes = 213 Bytes
                   = 13 bits
05. A virtual memory system uses 12 offset bits. What is the page size?
    w;:H u;l moaO;shla fkrefõ ìgq 12 la Ndú;d lrhs' msgq m%udKh l=ulao@
     2 offset bits = page size in bytes
                   = 212 Bytes
                   = 22 x 210 Bytes
                   = 4 KB
06. A virtual memory contains 4096 pages. How many bits are needed for the page number?
    w;:H u;lhl msgq 4096 la wvx.= fõ' msgq wxlh i|yd ìgq lShla wjYH fõo@
   No. of pages = 2 page number bits
                = 4096 = 212
                = 12 bits
07. A system uses 10 bits for the page number. How many pages are there?
    moaO;shla msgq wxlh i|yd ìgq 10 la Ndú;d lrhs' msgq lShla ;sfío@
    No. of pages = 2 page number bits
                 = 210
                 = 1024 pages

01. If a computer’s physical memory has 8,192 frames, each with a size of 4KB, what is the total memory
    capacity?
    mß.Klhl fN!;sl u;lfha tl tflys m%udKh 4KB jk rduq 8,192la ;sfí kï" iïmQ¾K u;l Odß;dj
    fldmuKo@
    Physical memory capacity = Size of one frame x number of frames
                              = 4 KB x 8192 = 22 x 210 Bytes x 213
                              = 212 x 213 Bytes = 25 x 220 Bytes
                              = 25 x Mega Bytes
                              = 32 MB
02. Consider a computer with an 8GB of memory that is divided into 65536 frames. Calculate the size of
    one frame.
    rduq 65536 lg fnod we;s 8GB u;lhla iys; mß.Klhla i,ld n,kak' tla rduqjl m%udKh .Kkh
    lrkak'
    Size of one frame = Physical memory capacity / number of frames
                      = 8 GB / 65536 = 23 x 230 Bytes / 216
                      = 23 x 230 Bytes / 210 x 23 x 23
                      = 210 x 210 Bytes / 23 = 27 x Kilo Bytes
                      = 128 KB
03. The size of a frame in a 4 GB physical memory is 256 KB. How many frames are there in this physical
    memory?
    4 GB fN!;sl u;lhl rduqjl úYd,;ajh 256 KB fõ' fuu fN!;sl u;lfha rduq lShla ;sfío@
    Number of frames = Physical memory capacity / Size of one frame
                    = 4 GB / 256 KB = 22 x 230 Bytes / 28 x 210 Bytes
                    = 22 x 220/ 28 = 22 x 210 x 22
                    = 24 x 210 = 214
                    = 16,384
04. What happens to the number of frames when the size of a frame is halved?
    rduqjl m%udKh wvlska wvq l< úg rduq .Kkg l=ula isÿfõo@
    Original frame size = 4 KB
    If halved → 2 KB
    Physical memory = 32 MB

    Number of frames = Physical memory / Frame size
                    = 32 MB / 2 KB
                    = 16384 frames

    Conclusion: When the frame size is halved, the number of frames is doubled.
    ks.ukh( rduq m%udKh wvlska wvq l< úg" rduq .Kk fo.=K fõ'

05. What happens to the number of frames when the size of a frame is doubled?
    rduqjl m%udKh fo.=K l< úg rduq .Kkg l=ula isÿfõo@
    Original frame size = 4 KB
    If doubled → 8 KB
    Physical memory = 32 MB

    Number of frames = Physical memory / Frame size
                    = 32 MB / 8 KB
                    = 4096 frames

    Conclusion: When the frame size is doubled, the number of frames is halved.
    ks.ukh( rduq m%udKh fo.=K l< úg" rduq .Kk wvlska wvq fõ'

Relationship between Pages and Frames                  NOTE 3
msgq iy rduq w;r iïnkaO;djh                            The size of a page must be equal to the size of
                                                       a frame to ensure proper placement in physical
NOTE 1                                                 memory. If not, it causes internal fragmentation.
The size of a frame and a page should always           fN!;sl u;lfha ksis f,i o;a; ;ekam;a lsÍu
be represented as a power of 2. Additionally,          iy;sl lsÍu i|yd msgj        q l m%udKh rduqjl
the number of pages and the number of frames           m%udKhg iudk úh hq;=h'tfia fkdue;s kï" th
                                                       wNHka;r LKavkSlrKhg fya;= fõ'
are also powers of 2, because Memory sizes
(virtual and physical) are powers of 2, and            Relationship Between Page and Frame

 01. A computer has a virtual memory of 1GB. The size of a frame is given as 128KB. How many
     pages could there be in the virtual memory?
     mß.Klhl w:;H u;lh 1GB fõ' rduqjl úYd,;ajh 128KB f,i olajd we;' w;:H u;lfha msgq
     lShla ;sìh yelso@

     Number of pages = Virtual memory capacity / Size of a page
                     = 1 GB / 128 KB = 230 Bytes / 27 x 210 Bytes
                     = 220 / 27 = 210 x 23
                     = 213
                     = 8192

 02. There are 1024 pages in a computer’s virtual memory. The size of a frame in the physical
     memory of this computer is 2 MB. If the capacity of the virtual memory is twice than that of the
     physical memory, how many frames are there in the physical memory?
     mß.Klhl w;:H u;lfha msgq 1024la we;' fuu mß.Klfha fN!;sl u;lfha we;s rduqjl
     úYd,;ajh 2 MB fõ' w;:H u;lfha Odß;dj fN!;sl u;lhg jvd fo.=Khla kï" fN!;sl u;lfha
     rduq lShla ;sfío@

     Virtual memory capacity = Size of a page x number of pages
                             = 2 MB x 1024
                             = 2 GB

     Number of frames = 1 GB / 2 MB
                     = 1024 MB / 2 MB
                     = 512

03. Consider a physical memory with 214 frames where the size of each frame is 4KB.
    tla tla rduqfõ m%udKh 4KB la jk rduq 214 la iys; fN!;sl u;lhla i,ld n,kak'

Number of frames = 214 = 16384

Calculate the total number of bits used for a physical address.
fN!;sl ,smskhla i|yd Ndú;d lrk uq¿ ìgq .Kk .Kkh lrkak'

We already know that the number of bits used for frame number is 14.
rduq wxlh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ìgq .Kk 14 nj wms oekgu;a oksuq'

2number of offset bits = The size of a frame
                      = 4 KB = 22 x 210 Bytes
                      = 212 Bytes = 212 Bytes
                      = 12

Therefore, total of 14 + 12 = 26 bits are used for a single physical address.
tneúka" tla fN!;sl ,smskhla i|yd uq¿ ìgq 14 ¬ 12 } 26 la Ndú;d fõ'

Get the physical address corresponding to the 31st byte in the 5th frame.
5 jk rduqj ;=< we;s 31 jk nhsghg wod, fN!;sl ,smskh ,nd .kak'

   According to the previous illustration
   of a frame, we can get an idea about

04. A computer has a 12-bit width address bus. In other words, the bus width of the computer is
    12.
    mß.Klhl ìgq 12 m<, ,smsk nihla we;' fjk;a jpk j,ska lsjfyd;a" mß.Klfha nia m<, 12
    ls'

If there are 16 frames in this physical memory, how many different addresses can one frame have?
fuu fN!;sl u;lfha rduq 16la ;sfí kï" tla rduqjlg tlsfklg fjkia ,smsk lShla ;sìh yelso@

First, we have to calculate the number of bits reserved for the frame number.
m<uqj" rduq wxlh i|yd fjka lr we;s ìgq .Kk .Kkh l< hq;=h'

2bits for the frame number = number of frames
                           = 16 = 24
                           =4

Now, we can find the number of bits used for the offset.
oeka" wmg wkq,ïNh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ,o ìgq .Kk fidhd.; yelsh'

Number of bits for the offset = Bust width – Number of bits for the frame number
                             = 12 – 4
                             =8

8 bits can have 28 different combinations.
ìgq 8lg tlsfklg fjkia ixfhdack 28la ;sìh yel'

Therefore, one frame can have 28 = 256 different addresses, meaning the size of a frame is 256
Bytes.
tneúka" tla rduqjl tlsfklg fjkia ,smsk 28 = 256 la ;sìh yel" tkï rduqjl úYd,;ajh nhsÜ 256
ls'

05. A physical memory with 8KB size-frames is divided into 16384 frames. This computer has a 30-
    bit virtual address space.
    8KB m%udKfha rduq iys; fN!;sl u;lhla rduq 16384lg fnod we;' fuu mß.Klhg ìgq 30l
    w:;H ,smsk wjldYhla we;'

How many pages are there in the virtual memory?
w;:H u;lfha msgq lShla ;sfío@

The size of a page is also 8KB since the sizes of frames and pages are the same.
rduq iy msgq j, m%udKh iudk neúka msgj   q l m%udKho 8KB fõ'

2number of bits for the offset = size of a page
                               = 8KB = 213
                               = 13

The number of bits used for the page number = 30 – 13 = 17
msgq wxlh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ,o ìgq .Kk

The number of pages in the virtual memory = 217 = 131,072
w;:H u;lfha we;s msgq .Kk

Calculate the capacity of the virtual memory
w;:H u;lfha Odß;dj .Kkh lrkak

Method 1:
Virtual memory capacity = 2the maximum length of a virtual address
Virtual memory capacity = 230 Bytes
Virtual memory capacity = 1 GB

Method 2:
Virtual memory capacity = size of one page x number of pages
Virtual memory capacity = 8KB x 217
Virtual memory capacity = 213 Bytes x 217
Virtual memory capacity = 230 Bytes
Virtual memory capacity = 1 GB

  06. A computer has a physical memory of X GB.
      mß.Klhlg X GB l fN!;sl u;lhla we;'

  This computer has an equal number of bits reserved for both the frame number and offset. If the
  above physical memory has been divided into 16384 frames, what is X?
  fuu mß.Klfha rduq wxlh iy wkq,ïNh hk folgu iudk ìgq ixLHdjla fjka lr we;' by; fN!;sl
  u;lh rduq 16384lg fnod we;akï X hkq l=ulao@

  2bits for the frame number = Number of frames
                             = 16384 = 214
                             = 14

  Since both the frame number and offset have an equal number of bits reserved for them, offset bits
  are also 14.
  rduq wxlh iy wkq,ïNh hk folgu iudk ìgq ixLHdjla fjka lr we;s neúka" wkq,ïN ìgq o 14 ls'

  So, a physical address of this computer contains a total of 14 + 14 = 28 bits i.e. the bus width is
  28.
  tneúka" fuu mß.Klfha fN!;sl ,smskhl uq¿ ìgq 14 ¬ 14 } 28 la wvx.= fõ " tkï nia m<, 28ls'

  X = 2bus width
  X = 228 Bytes
  X = 210 x 210 x 28 Bytes
  X = 210 x 28 KB
  X = 28 MB
  X = 256 MB

 Q – Suppose a computer’s CPU has a logical address space of 14 bits. 4 bits are used for the page
 number and the other 10 bits are used for the displacement. The computer uses 13 bits to identify
 a frame in the physical memory.
 mß.Klhl CPU tlg ìgq 14l ;d¾lsl ,smsk wjldYhla we;ehs is;uq' msgq wxlh i|yd ìgq 4 la Ndú;d
 lrk w;r wfkla ìgq 10 úia:dmkh i|yd Ndú;d fõ' fN!;sl u;lfha rduqjla y÷kd .ekSug mß.Klh
 ìgq 13la Ndú;d lrhs'

 1.   How many bits are used for the frame number?
      rduq wxlh i|yd ìgq fldmuKla Ndú;d fõo@

      As we learned earlier, we know that the number of bits used for the offset in a page and the
      number of bits used for offset in frame are identical.
      wm l,ska bf.k .;a mßÈ" msgj  q l úia:dmkh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ìgq .Kk iy rduqjl úia:dmkh
      i|yd Ndú;d lrk ìgq .Kk iudk nj wms oksuq'

      Therefore, if the frame has 13 bits for both the page number and offset, 10 bits from it should
      be reserved for the offset part.
      tneúka" rduqfjys msgq wxlh iy úia:dmkh hk folgu ìgq 13la ;sfí kï" thska ìgq 10la
      úia:dmk fldgi i|yd fjka l< hq;=h'

      Hence, the number of bits used for the frame number should be 13-10 = 3.
      tneúka" rduq wxlh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ìgq .Kk 13-10 = 3 úh hq;=h'

       fma<sfha j,x.= Ndjh ms<sìUq lrhs'

   w;r túg th wod< fN!;sl ,smskh njg m;ajkq we;'

3.   Assume that there was a request for the virtual address 01000000001011. In order to fulfill the
     request, the operating system changed the present/absent bit of page 7 from 1 to 0. Now,
     what will be the 13-bit physical address that corresponds to the
     virtual address 01000000001011?
     01000000001011 w:;H ,smskh i|yd b,a,Sula we;s nj Wml,amkh lrkak' b,a,Su bgq
     lsÍu i|yd" fufyhqï moaO;sh úiska 7 jk msgqfõ we;$ke; ìgqj tl 1 isg 0 olajd fjkia lf<ah'
     oeka" w;:H ,smskh jk 01000000001011 g wkqrEmKh jk ìgq 13lska hq;a fN!;sl ,smskh l=ula
     fõúo@

                                             If we look at the page table, we can see that the page

                                             jkafka tys we;$ke; ìgqj 0 ùuhs'

     fõ'

     fufyhqï moaO;sh 7 jk msgqfõ we;$ke; ìgqj 1 isg 0 olajd fjkia l< nj wmg mjid we;'

     It means that page 7 was previously in frame 1012 (according to the page table) and now

     mQrKh lr .; yel'

     oeka wmg ysia rduqjla we;s w;r 4 jk msgj
                                            q tu rduqjg mQrKh l< yel'

4.    What are the most-likely reasons for a page fault to occur?
      Page fault tlla isÿùug fndfyda ÿrg bv we;s fya;= fudkjdo@

      other pages.

05. A computer that uses 32-bit virtual address space has frames of 2MB. There are total of 512
frames in this computer. When the application speed.exe is being executed, a part of the page
table looks as follows.
32-bit w;:H ,smsk wjldYhla Ndú;d lrk mß.Klhl 2MB m%udKfha rduq we;' fuu mß.Klfha
rduq 512 la we;' speed.exe hk fhÿu l%shd;aul jk úg" msgq j.=fõ fldgila my; mßÈ Èiafõ'

     Page number       Frame number         Present/Absent bit
          0              000001110                     1
          1              000000001                     1
          2              000000101                     1
          3              000001000                     1
          4              000100000                     1
          5              000000000                     0
          6              000000011                     1
          7              000000111                     1

Notes:
The frame number is given in binary.
rduq wxlh oaúuh f,iska ,nd § we;'

The present/absent bit indicates whether a page is valid and can be used by the CPU. If it is 1, it is
valid, if it is 0, it is not valid and a page fault occurs.
we;$ke; ìgqj uÕska msgj        q la j,x.=o iy CPU úiska Ndú;d l< yelso hkak olajhs' th 1 kï th j,x.=
fõ" th 0 kï" th j,x.= fkdjk w;r msgq fodaIhla we;sfõ'

1.    Calculate the total RAM capacity of this computer.
      fuu mß.Klfha iïmQ¾K RAM Odß;dj .Kkh lrkak'

      We can use the following formula to calculate the RAM capacity.
      RAM Odß;dj .Kkh lsÍug my; iQ;%h Ndú;d l< yel'

      Total size of the physical memory = Number of frames x Size of one frame
      fN!;sl u;lfha iïmQ¾K m%udKh = rduq .Kk x tla rduqjl m%udKh

      Therefore, the total RAM capacity = 512 x 2MB = 1024 MB = 1 GB
      tneúka" iïmQ¾K RAM Odß;dj

                                                                                                    ictfromabc.com

2.   Calculate the number of bits used for the page number.
     msgq wxlh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ìgq .Kk .Kkh lrkak'

     Size of a page = Size of a page
     Size of a page = 2 MB

     2number of offset bits = Size of a page
     2number of offset bits = 2 MB
     2number of offset bits = 21 x 220 Bytes
     2number of offset bits = 221
     Number of offset bits = 21

     The length of a virtual address = Page number bits + offset bits
     Page number bits = The length of a virtual address – offset bits
     Page number bits = 32 – 21
     Page number bits = 11

3.   Imagine that the CPU wants to access the virtual address 00000000110
     000010110100010001010. What physical address would this virtual address be mapped to?
     CPU yg 00000000110 000010110100010001010 hk w;:H ,smskhg m%fõY ùug wjYH nj is;kak'
     fuu w;:H ,smskh wkqrEmKh jkafka l=uk fN!;sl ,smskhlgo@

     In the previous section, we calculated that the number of bits used for the page number is 11.
     Therefore, the first 11 bits of this virtual address should be the page number and the rest 21
     bits should be the offset.
     fmr fldgfia§ wm úiska msgq wxlh i|yd Ndú;d lrk ,o ìgq .Kk 11 la nj .Kkh lrk ,È'
     tu ksid fuu w:;H ,smskfha uq,a ìgq 11 msgq wxlh úh hq;= w;r b;sß ìgq 21 úia:dmkh úh hq;=h'

     Page number – 00000000110 - 610
     msgq wxlh

     If we look at the page table, we can see that the present/absent bit of page 6 is 1, which means
     that this page is valid.
     wm msgq j.=j foi ne¨jfyd;a" 6 jk msgqfõ we;$ke; ìgqj 1 jk nj wmg fmfka" thska woyia
     jkafka fuu msgj q j,x.= njhs'

     Frame 000000011 which is 3 in decimal, is the corresponding frame number of page 6.
     Therefore, we can replace the page number part by this 9-bit length frame number to form the
     physical address.
     oYu mdofhka 3 jk rduqj 000000011 hkq msgq wxl 6 g wkqrEm rduq wxlhhs' tuksid" fN!;sl ,smskh
     iE§u i|yd wmg msgq wxl fldgi fuu ìgq 9lska È. rduq wxlfhka m%;sia:dmkh l< yelsh'

     Note that the offset part remains intact.
     úia;dmk fldgi fkdfjkiaj mj;sk nj igyka lr.kak'

06. Imagine the CPU wants to access the virtual address 0x4F5DE. If the frame number that
corresponds to the page number 0x4F, is 0x35, what is the physical address that maps to 0x4F5DE?
CPU yg 0x4F5DE hk w:;H ,smskh fj; m%fõY ùug wjYH nj is;kak' msgq wxl jk 0x4F g wkqrEm
jk rduq wxlh 0x35 kï" 0x4F5DE fj; wkqrEm jk fN!;sl ,smskh l=ulao@

Hint: 0x is the prefix of a hexadecimal number
bÕsh( 0x hkq Iâ oYu ixLHdjl Wmi¾.hhs

1) 0x354F5DE             2) 0x355DE                3) 0x4F355DE
4) 0x5DE                 5) None of the above

Answer: 2)

Let’s consider the virtual address given in hexadecimal notation.
Iâ oYuh wxlkfhka § we;s w;:H ,smskh i,ld n,uq'

0x4F5DE

0x – prefix
4F5DE – virtual address in hexadecimal w;;H ,smskh Iâ oYuh wdldrfhka
0100 1111 0101 1101 1110 – virtual address in binary w;;H ,smskh oaùuh wdldrfhka

It is given that the page number of this virtual address is 0x4F.
fuu w;:H ,smskfha msgq wxlh 0x4F nj ,nd § we;'

Therefore, the first 8 bits of the address is used for the page number and the rest of the bits are
used for the offset.
tuksid" ,smskfha uq,a ìgq 8 msgq wxlh i|yd Ndú;d lrk w;r b;sß ìgq úia:dmkh i|yd Ndú;d fõ'

It is also given that the frame number that corresponds to this page is 0x35. So, it simply means
that the page 0x4F maps to frame 0x35.
fuu msgj  q g wkqrEm jk rduq wxlh 0x35 nj o ,nd § we;' tneúka" tu.ska ir,j woyia lrkafka 0x4F
hk msgj  q 0x35 hk rduqjg wkqrEm jk njhs'

To obtain the physical address for the given virtual address, we can replace the page number by the
frame number while preserving the offset part intact.
,nd § we;s w:;H ,smskh i|yd jk fN!;sl ,smskh ,nd .ekSu i|yd" wmg msgq wxlh rduq wxlfhka
m%;sia:dmkh l< yelsh' tA w;ru úia:dmkh fldgi fkdfjkiaj mj;ajd .; yel'

Therefore, the physical address that corresponds to the virtual address 0x4F5DE should be as
follows.
tneúka" 0x4F5DE hk w:;H ,smskhg wkqrEm jk fN!;sl ,smskh my; mßÈ úh hq;=h'

0x355DE

Calculating the size of a page table
msgq j.=jl úYd,;ajh .Kkh lsÍu

  Page Table Size = Page Table Entry (PTE) Count x Size of One Page Table Entry
  msgq j.=fõ m%udKh = PTE .Kk x PTE tll m%udKh

Page Table Entry (PTE)                                      Page Table Entry Size
A small record inside a page table used by an               Indicates how many bytes are required to store
operating system to manage virtual memory.                  one entry in the page table, including
w:;H u;lh l<ukdlrKh lsÍu i|yd                               information such as the physical frame address
fufyhqï moaO;shla úiska Ndú;d lrk msgq j.=jla
;=< we;s l=vd jd¾;djls'                                     and control bits
                                                            fN!;sl rduq ,smskh iy md,k ìgq jeks f;dr;=re
It tells the computer how a virtual memory page             we;=¿j" msgq j.=fõ tla we;=<;a lsÍula .nvd
maps to physical memory.                                    lsÍug fldmuK nhsÜ m%udKhla wjYH oehs

Page Table Entry Count
                                                              PTE úYd,;ajh = rduq wxl ìgq + wfkl=;a ìgq
Indicates how many virtual memory pages are
mapped or can be mapped to physical memory.
fN!;sl u;lhg is;shï.; l< yels fyda
is;shï.; l< yels w;:H u;l msgq .Kk olajhs'

Each entry in the count represents one page of
virtual memory that the operating system can
manage.
.Kka lsÍfï iEu we;=<;a lsÍulau fufyhqï
moaO;shg l<ukdlrKh l< yels w;:H u;lfha
tla msgjq la ksfhdackh lrhs'

  PTE Count = Number of pages
  PTE .Kk = msgq .Kk

  Virtual Address Space: 4 GB (232 bytes)
  Page Size: 4 KB (212 bytes)
  Size of Each PTE: 4 bytes (32 bits, assuming typical 32-bit PTE)

  Q - Calculate the total size of the page table.
  msgq j.=fõ iïmQ¾K úYd,;ajh .Kkh lrkak'

  Number of Virtual Pages (N):
  w;;H msgq .Kk (N):

  N = Virtual address space / page size
    = 232 bytes / 212 bytes = 220 pages

  Size of each PTE is 4 bytes
  msgq j.=fõ tla tla fma<sfha úYd,;ajh nhsg 4ls'

  Number of PTEs in the page table = Number of virtual pages = 220 PTEs
  Total page table size = Number of PTEs x Size of each PTE
                         = 220 x 4 bytes = (220 x 4) / 220 MB
                         = 4 MB
